% JAC1964-20261121. /root/arithmetic_sign.
\subsection*{AR-15a. The algebraic radical and a precise grade-zero obstruction}

\begin{proposition}[AR-15a]
Let \(J\), \(N\), and \(\mathcal A_F\) be as in AR-15 for the Fermat
threefold. Suppose that, for every Jacobian \(B\) over a finite extension
of \(F\), the ordinary cycle-class map is injective on
\[
             \operatorname{CH}^2_{(0)}(B\times J)_{\mathbf Q}.
             \tag{AR15a.1}
\]
Then \(\mathcal A_F=0\). More precisely, any nonzero algebraically
trivial class in \(\operatorname{CH}^2(N_F)\) would produce, over a
finite extension, a nonzero homologically trivial class in
\(\operatorname{CH}^2_{(0)}(B\times J)_{\mathbf Q}\) for some Jacobian
\(B\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Use the curve-family expression in the proof of AR-15. If its sum is
nonzero, at least one projected summand
\(\Gamma_*D\), with \(D\in\operatorname{Pic}^0(C)_{\mathbf Q}\),
is nonzero. Insert the degree-one Chow projector of \(C\), since it
acts identically on \(D\), and the projector of \(N\) on the target.
The resulting correspondence is nonzero because its action on \(D\)
is nonzero.

Let \(B=\operatorname{Jac}(C)\) and \(b=\dim B\). The actual isomorphism
\(h^1(C)\simeq h^1(B)\) transports this correspondence to a nonzero
\[
 \Gamma_B\in\operatorname{CH}^{b+1}(B\times J)_{\mathbf Q},
 \qquad
 \Gamma_B=\pi_N\circ\Gamma_B\circ\pi_1^B.
 \tag{AR15a.2}
\]
Here the codimension is \(b+1\), because the induced cohomological
map raises degree one on \(B\) to degree three on \(J\).
Its cohomological realization is zero: a nonzero map
\(H^1(B)\to H(N)(1)\) would have a nonzero image of types
\((1,0),(0,1)\), while \(H(N)(1)\) has types
\((2,-1),(-1,2)\).

As a cycle on the product, (AR15a.2) is supported in the Chow--K\"unneth
component
\[
        \pi_{2b-1}^B\otimes\pi_3^J.
\]
The first index is \(2b-1\), not one, because the source projector
is transposed when a correspondence is viewed as a cycle on the
product. Thus the total index is \(2b+2=2(b+1)\).
Equivalently \(\Gamma_B\) belongs to Beauville grade zero.

Use the product principal polarization to identify the dual of
\(B\times J\) with \(B\times J\). The Fourier isomorphism on this
abelian variety, of dimension \(b+3\), sends a grade-zero class of codimension \(b+1\)
to one of codimension
\[
             (b+3)-(b+1)=2.
\]
It preserves homological triviality and is invertible on rational
Chow groups. Therefore the Fourier transform of \(\Gamma_B\) is a
nonzero homologically trivial class in the space in (AR15a.1), as
claimed. Under the displayed injectivity hypothesis this is
impossible, proving the proposition.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Status and implication.}
The Fourier isomorphism and the degree-one Chow-motive isomorphism
are the same established inputs used in AR-1a and AR-2.
Injectivity in (AR15a.1) is stated here as an additional hypothesis;
it has not been deduced from CM Hodge algebraicity, numerical
positivity, Kimura nilpotence, or the new Selmer bound.
This proposition gives a concrete obstruction to constructing a
nonzero algebraically trivial zero-height direction in \(N\):
such a direction would also violate grade-zero cycle-class
injectivity on an explicit abelian product. It does not prove
that the remaining Griffiths quotient is generated by the
positive CM orbit, or that its real height is positive.
% JAC1964-20261121. /root/programme_audit; GPT-6 Astra Pro.
% New deduction from the actual projector P13/P17 and curve parameters.
\subsection*{JAC-P18. A fourfold obstruction to an algebraic Fermat radical}

\begin{proposition}[JAC-P18]\label{jac:P18}
Let $F$ be a number field over which the actual Fermat summand
$N=(J,\pi_N)$ of JAC-P13 and JAC-P17 is defined. In particular one
may take $F=\mathbf Q$ or $\mathbf Q(i)$. Put
\[
 \mathcal A_F=\{z\in\operatorname{CH}^2(N_F)_{\mathbf Q}:
       z_{\overline F}\text{ is algebraically trivial on }
       J_{\overline F}\}.
\]
If $\mathcal A_F\ne0$, there are a finite extension $F'/F$, a
smooth projective geometrically integral curve $C/F'$ with a point
$c_0\in C(F')$, and a nonzero cycle
\[
 Z\in\operatorname{CH}^2(C\times J_{F'})_{\mathbf Q}
\]
such that
\[
  Z=(\pi_1^C\otimes\pi_N)_*Z,
       \qquad \operatorname{cl}(Z)=0.
                                                        \tag{P18.1}
\]
Here
$\pi_1^C=\Delta_C-[c_0]\times C-C\times[c_0]$,
and homological triviality holds for Betti cohomology under every
complex embedding and for every $\ell$-adic realization.
The cycle $Z$ belongs to the degree-four component of the product
Chow--K\"unneth decomposition on the fourfold $C\times J_{F'}$:
\[
                 (\pi_4^{C\times J})_*Z=Z.             \tag{P18.2}
\]

Consequently, injectivity of the ordinary cycle-class map on
$(\pi_1^C\otimes\pi_N)_*\operatorname{CH}^2(C\times J_{F'})_{
\mathbf Q}$ for every such $F'$ and $C$ implies
$\mathcal A_F=0$. Murre's conjecture~(D) in codimension two for
these product decompositions is a sufficient condition. No
converse and no injectivity on the full group
$\operatorname{CH}^2(N_F)_{\mathbf Q}$ is asserted.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Take $0\ne z\in\mathcal A_F$. Algebraic equivalence is generated
by differences of fibres of cycle families over smooth projective
curves. Thus over $\overline F$ there is an equality
\[
 z_{\overline F}=\sum_j(\Gamma_j)_*D_j,
 \qquad
 \Gamma_j\in\operatorname{CH}^2(C_j\times J)_{\mathbf Q},
 \quad D_j\in\operatorname{Pic}^0(C_j)_{\mathbf Q}.
                                                        \tag{P18.3}
\]
One may take each $D_j$ to be a rational linear combination of
differences of rational points. There are only finitely many
curves, cycles, points and rational equivalences in this
expression. Enlarge $F$ by a finite extension $F'$ so that all
are defined over $F'$, all the curves are smooth projective and
geometrically integral, and a chosen basepoint on each curve is
$F'$-rational. The equality then holds over $F'$ after enlarging
$F'$ once more if necessary. This last assertion follows directly
from the finite data in a rational equivalence, or from the
direct-limit description of Chow groups under algebraic field
extension.

Rational restriction from $F$ to $F'$ is injective, because its
composition with norm is multiplication by $[F':F]$.
Hence $z_{F'}\ne0$. Apply $\pi_N$ to (P18.3). Since
$(\pi_N)_*z_{F'}=z_{F'}$, at least one summand
$\pi_N\circ\Gamma_j$ has nonzero action on $D_j$. Choose that
summand, and write its curve, divisor and correspondence as
$C,D,\Gamma$.

The projector $\pi_1^C$ acts identically on
$\operatorname{Pic}^0(C)_{\mathbf Q}$. Define the actual Chow
correspondence
\[
              Z=\pi_N\circ\Gamma\circ\pi_1^C.
                                                        \tag{P18.4}
\]
It still has codimension two on $C\times J$: a correspondence
from the curve to the threefold that takes divisors to
codimension-two cycles has precisely this codimension.
Its action on $D$ is the chosen nonzero summand, so $Z\ne0$ as
a rational Chow class. This uses its actual action, not its
numerical or cohomological realization.

When composition of correspondences is viewed as an action on
the product cycle, the source projector is transposed:
\[
 \pi_N\circ\Gamma\circ\pi_1^C
       =({}^t\pi_1^C\otimes\pi_N)_*\Gamma.
\]
For the displayed degree-one curve projector
${}^t\pi_1^C=\pi_1^C$. Idempotence of both projectors proves
the first identity in (P18.1).

Fix an embedding $F'\hookrightarrow\mathbf C$. The cohomology
class of $Z$ therefore belongs to
\[
 H^1(C,\mathbf Q)\otimes H^3(N,\mathbf Q)
       \ \subset\ H^4(C\times J,\mathbf Q).
\]
The two factors have Hodge types $(1,0),(0,1)$ and
$(3,0),(0,3)$, respectively. Their tensor product has only types
$(4,0),(3,1),(1,3),(0,4)$ and has no $(2,2)$ part. An algebraic
codimension-two cycle has type $(2,2)$, so
$\operatorname{cl}(Z)=0$. The same argument works for every
embedding. Compatibility of Betti and $\ell$-adic cycle classes
gives the asserted vanishing in every $\ell$-adic realization.

The product decomposition has
\[
 \pi_4^{C\times J}
   =\pi_0^C\otimes\pi_4^J
      +\pi_1^C\otimes\pi_3^J
      +\pi_2^C\otimes\pi_2^J.
\]
JAC-P13/P17 gives
$\pi_N\pi_3^J=\pi_3^J\pi_N=\pi_N$ in rational Chow
correspondences. Orthogonality now proves (P18.2).
Murre~(D) in this codimension says that
$\ker(\pi_4^{C\times J})_*$ is exactly the homologically
trivial subgroup. It would force the nonzero $Z$ simultaneously
to be killed and fixed by $\pi_4^{C\times J}$, a contradiction.
The weaker stated injectivity on the single component gives the
same contradiction and proves the sufficient condition.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Source and logical scope.}
The actual projector and the extreme Hodge types are the proved
JAC-P13/P17 inputs. Curve parameterization of algebraic
equivalence is used in its usual form, as in Achter--Casalaina-Martin--Vial,
\emph{Parameter spaces for algebraic equivalence},
Proposition~3.5 and Lemma~3.9, author pp.~4--7,
\url{https://www.math.uni-bielefeld.de/~vial/abtriv.pdf}.
The required product assertion is not supplied by the theorem of
Xu--Xu: \emph{Remarks on Murre's conjecture on Chow groups},
Corollary~3.9, author p.~9, proves (A) and (B) for an abelian
threefold times a curve;
\url{https://arxiv.org/pdf/1110.0668}.
Kenichiro Kimura, \emph{Murre's conjectures for certain product
varieties}, author p.~2 and Theorem~2.1, p.~3, explicitly isolates
the related degree-four component and treats restricted support
types under additional hypotheses;
\url{https://arxiv.org/pdf/math/0509249}.
Those hypotheses do not cover $C\times E_i^3$.
The obstruction here concerns rational Chow faithfulness in one
degree-four component; it must not be confused with the standard
conjecture identifying homological and numerical equivalence.
It refines AR-15a by keeping the obstructing variety four-dimensional.
\section{AR-19. A direct Katz nonvanishing calculation at
5}\label{ar-19.-a-direct-katz-nonvanishing-calculation-at-5}

Run \texttt{JAC1964-20261121}; \path{/root/arithmetic_sign}, GPT-6
Astra Pro; effort setting not exposed. This is a new deduction from the
explicitly cited Katz-measure construction. The complete proof and exact
script have passed independent whole-read checks by the root and h9
agents. It establishes a p-adic analytic input, not injectivity of a
Chow Abel--Jacobi map.

\subsection{Statement and conventions}\label{statement-and-conventions}

Put \(K=\mathbf Q(i)\), \(\mathfrak f=(4)\), and let \(\psi\) be the
ideal-valued Grossencharacter of \[
E:\quad y^2=x^3+x,\qquad \omega=\frac{dx}{2y}.
\] Thus \(\psi\) is the character denoted \(\psi'\) in BDP §5.2, its
conductor is \((4)\), and \(\psi\bar\psi=\mathbf N\). At the split
ordinary prime \(p=5\), define the two ideal-valued characters \[
\rho=\frac{\bar\psi^2}{\psi},\qquad
\rho_0=\frac{\psi^3}{\bar\psi^2}.
\tag{AR19.1}
\] We use the Bannai--Kobayashi (BK) convention: their Katz value
\(L_{p,\mathfrak f}(\rho_0)\) interpolates \(L(\rho_0^{-1},0)\), and
\(\rho_0\) has infinity type \((3,-2)\) in their convention. Castella's
infinity-type convention is opposite for the same ideal-valued
character, and his Katz function interpolates \(L(\xi,0)\). Thus the
relevant comparison is \(\xi=\rho^{-1}\). In particular \[
\rho^{-1}=\psi^3\mathbf N^{-2}=\chi\mathbf N^{-2},
\] where \(\chi\) is the weight-four Fermat character of AR-11.

\textbf{Proposition AR-19.} With compatible good-reduction CM periods,
the BK value \(L_{p,(4)}(\rho)\) is a 5-adic unit. Consequently the Katz
nonvanishing premise (AR-KATZ) holds: \[
\mathcal L_v(\chi\mathbf N^{-2})(0)\ne0.
\tag{AR19.2}
\] The statement concerns the Katz function itself and does not use the
ramified Rankin--Katz comparison factor in BDP §5.2.

\subsection{1. The exact fourth-order theta
coefficient}\label{the-exact-fourth-order-theta-coefficient}

Write \(E(\mathbf C)=\mathbf C/\Gamma\), \(\Gamma=4\Omega\mathbf Z[i]\),
using the differential \(\omega\), and put
\(A=\operatorname{area}(\mathbf C/\Gamma)/\pi\). The modified weight-two
Eisenstein constant vanishes for a square lattice: multiplication by
\(i\) fixes the lattice and multiplies that constant by \(i^{-2}=-1\).
Hence the reduced theta function is the Weierstrass sigma function with
derivative one at zero.

For a nonzero torsion point \(P=z_P\bmod\Gamma\), put \[
F(P)=\zeta(z_P)-\frac{\bar z_P}{A},\qquad
x=\wp(z_P),\qquad y=\frac{\wp'(z_P)}2.
\] The translated Kronecker function in BK equation (15), p.15, has the
expansion \[
\begin{aligned}
\Theta_{P,0}(z,w)&=\frac1w+F(z+P)
 +\frac{F(z+P)^2-\wp(z+P)}2w\\
&\quad +\frac{F(z+P)^3-3F(z+P)\wp(z+P)-\wp'(z+P)}6w^2+O(w^3).
\end{aligned}
\] where in this formula \(F(z+P)=\zeta(z+z_P)-\bar z_P/A\) is
holomorphic in \(z\); the conjugate of the variable \(z\) is not
subtracted. Its derivatives satisfy \[
F'=-x,\qquad x'=2y,\qquad y'=3x^2+1.
\] By BK Theorem 1.17, p.17, comparison of the coefficient of \(z^2w^2\)
gives \[
\mathcal E(P):=\frac{e^*_{2,3}(P,0)}{A^2}
 =-F(P)(2x^2+1)-y\bigl(F(P)^2+x\bigr).
\tag{AR19.3}
\] Indeed applying the derivation \(D(F)=-x\), \(D(x)=2y\),
\(D(y)=3x^2+1\) twice to \((F^3-3Fx-2y)/6\) gives the right side. The
two factorials give
\(\partial_z^2\partial_w^2\Theta_{P,0}(0,0)=2\mathcal E(P)\).

Choose a primitive \(4\)-torsion generator with \(2P=(i,0)\). Its two
conjugates over \(K\) can be written \[
P_\pm=\bigl(i(1\pm\sqrt2),\ (1\pm\sqrt2)(1-i)\bigr).
\tag{AR19.4}
\] The duplication formula verifies \(2P_\pm=(i,0)\); adding
\((-i,0)=i(2P_+)\) to \(P_+\) gives \(P_-\). Thus \(P_-=[1+2i]P_+\). The
reduced zeta function vanishes at a nonzero half-period, so its
duplication formula gives \[
F(P_\pm)=-\frac{3x(P_\pm)^2+1}{4y(P_\pm)}.
\] Substitute (AR19.4) into (AR19.3). Using only \(i^2=-1\) and
\((\sqrt2)^2=2\), one obtains the exact identities \[
\mathcal E(P_+)=\mathcal E(P_-)=-\frac{1+i}{4},
\qquad
\mathcal E(P_+)+\mathcal E(P_-)=-\frac{1+i}{2}.
\tag{AR19.5}
\] Changing a lattice generator by a Gaussian unit multiplies this sum
by a Gaussian unit and cannot affect the ensuing nonvanishing. Among the
two points in (AR19.4), at least one is represented by a lattice
generator divided by four: the primitive classes of
\(\mathbf Z[i]/(4)\), modulo global Gaussian units, are precisely its
two ray classes. Both points give the same coefficient, so that choice
is immaterial.

\subsection{2. The two ray classes and their measure
normalizations}\label{the-two-ray-classes-and-their-measure-normalizations}

The ray class group modulo \((4)\) has order two:
\(|(\mathbf Z[i]/4)^\times|=8\) and the four global units inject. Use
inverse ideal representatives \(1\) and \(\mathfrak a^{-1}\), with \[
\mathfrak a=(\beta),\qquad \beta=1+6i,\qquad N\mathfrak a=37.
\] Both are prime to \(5\mathfrak f\), and \(\beta\equiv1+2i\pmod4\).
For this curve \(\psi(\mathfrak a)=\beta\). This also follows from the
usual primary-generator formula \(\psi=\psi_{y^2=x^3-x}\mu\): \(-\beta\)
is primary and the quadratic character of \(K(\sqrt2)/K\) has value
\(-1\) at norm 37. The resulting isogeny scalar in BK §3.3 is \[
\Lambda(\mathfrak a)=\psi(\mathfrak a)=\beta.
\] The ray class field is \(K(\sqrt2)\). We may take the same equation
of \(E\) on that field; all its conjugates have the same differential
and period lattice. BK §3.3, pp.40--42, then gives \[
\Omega_p^{\sigma_{\mathfrak a}}
 =\frac{\Lambda(\mathfrak a)}{N\mathfrak a}\Omega_p,
\qquad \Omega_p\in W(\overline{\mathbf F}_5)^\times.
\tag{AR19.6}
\] The choices \(\alpha_0=1\in\mathfrak a^{-1}\cap P_K(\mathfrak f)\) in
their Definition 3.9 give translated points \(\Omega\) and
\(\Lambda(\mathfrak a)\Omega\). These are \(P_+,P_-\), up to the same
harmless choice of Gaussian unit.

For any such representative \(\mathfrak b^{-1}\), BK Definitions
3.8--3.9, equations (38)--(39), and the displayed formula following
Definition 3.9, pp.41--42, yield at \(\rho_0\) (with \(k=3,l=2\)) \[
L_{p,\mathfrak f}(\rho_0,\mathfrak b^{-1})
 =\Omega_p\rho_0(\mathfrak b)^{-1}(N\mathfrak b)^2(N\mathfrak f)^2
 \int_{\mathbf Z_5^\times\times\mathbf Z_5^\times}
       x^2y^2\,d\mu_{\Omega,0}(x,y;\mathfrak b^{-1}\Gamma).
\tag{AR19.7}
\] These measures are integral; this is Corollary 2.17, p.30, and
Definition 3.2, p.36, applied to prime-to-5 torsion on the good model.
By the lattice-scaling definition of the measure on p.41 and (AR19.6),
its moment over all of \(\mathbf Z_5^2\) is \[
\int_{\mathbf Z_5^2}x^2y^2\,d\mu_{\Omega,0}(x,y;\mathfrak b^{-1}\Gamma)
 =2\Lambda(\mathfrak b)
       (\Omega_p^{\sigma_{\mathfrak b}})^4
       \mathcal E\bigl(\Lambda(\mathfrak b)\Omega\bigr).
\tag{AR19.8}
\] Although the second torsion argument is zero, BK's regularization
subtracts a term depending only on the second formal variable. Two
derivatives in the first variable kill it, so it makes no contribution
to this moment.

The multiplier of \(2\Omega_p^5(N\mathfrak f)^2\mathcal E\) after
substituting (AR19.8) in (AR19.7) is exactly \[
\frac{\Lambda(\mathfrak b)^5}
 {\rho_0(\mathfrak b)(N\mathfrak b)^2}=1,
\tag{AR19.9}
\] since \(\Lambda=\psi\),
\(N\mathfrak b=\psi(\mathfrak b)\bar\psi(\mathfrak b)\), and
\(\rho_0=\psi^3/\bar\psi^2\).

\subsection{3. The unit congruence and the noncritical
value}\label{the-unit-congruence-and-the-noncritical-value}

On the complement of \(\mathbf Z_5^\times\times\mathbf Z_5^\times\) in
\(\mathbf Z_5^2\), the function \(x^2y^2\) is divisible by \(25\).
Integrality of the measure therefore permits replacing the unit-domain
moment in (AR19.7) by (AR19.8) modulo 5. Every exterior factor in
(AR19.7) is a unit. Since \(N\mathfrak f=16\), summing the two classes
and using (AR19.5) gives \[
L_{p,(4)}(\rho_0)
 \equiv2\Omega_p^5\,16^2
          \left(-\frac{1+i}{2}\right)
 =-256(1+i)\Omega_p^5\pmod5.
\tag{AR19.10}
\] This is a unit at either prime over 5: \(1+i\) has norm two, and
\(\Omega_p\) is a unit.

On the Galois group of the ray-class tower of conductor
\(4\cdot5^\infty\), both \(\psi\) and \(\bar\psi\), interpreted as
5-adic characters in the BK convention, take values in
\(\mathbf Z_5^\times\). Therefore \[
\frac{\rho_0}{\rho}
 =\left(\frac{\psi}{\bar\psi}\right)^4
 \equiv1\pmod5.
\tag{AR19.11}
\] Integrality of the Katz measure gives \[
L_{p,(4)}(\rho)\equiv L_{p,(4)}(\rho_0)\not\equiv0\pmod5.
\tag{AR19.12}
\] This proves the BK unit assertion without a numerical approximation.

Finally choose the \textbf{same compatible complex and 5-adic period
data} in BK and Castella, attached to the good equation and differential
fixed above. Compare BK Theorem 3.11, pp.44--46, with Castella Theorem
2.2.1, pp.7--8. Under \(\xi=\rho^{-1}\), their interpolation values
involve the same Hecke value, gamma factor, and the two Euler factors at
5. BK writes one factor explicitly and the other in
\(L_{\mathfrak f\bar{\mathfrak p}}\). In particular \(\rho_0^{-1}\) has
Castella infinity type \((3,-2)\), so that the displayed formulas match
literally with this period choice. More generally they match on the
critical characters in the component with \(k\equiv3\pmod4\),
\(l\equiv2\pmod4\); the BK sign \((-1)^{k+l-1}\) is then +1. These
critical characters are dense in that two-variable component. The
uniqueness of the integral Katz measure therefore identifies the two
functions there with this inverse-character convention. Both \(\rho\)
and \(\rho_0\) lie in the component by (AR19.11). This proves (AR19.2)
from (AR19.12). Changing the good integral period data multiplies the
evaluation at this fixed algebraic weight by a nonzero unit, so
nonvanishing is independent of that permitted choice; no assertion about
an arbitrary weight-dependent global analytic rescaling is needed.

\subsection{Scope}\label{scope}

AR-19 supplies the previously missing nonzero Katz value for the Fermat
character at 5. Together with a separately checked Euler-system and
duality argument at the actual conductor, it can supply a
Selmer-dimension bound. It does not give scalar-extended étale
Abel--Jacobi injectivity, does not identify a Selmer dimension with a
Chow dimension, and does not prove real-height factorization through a
p-adic Abel--Jacobi map.

\subsection{Primary locators and computational
check}\label{primary-locators-and-computational-check}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Bannai--Kobayashi, \emph{Algebraic theta functions and the p-adic
  interpolation of Eisenstein--Kronecker numbers}, arXiv:math/0610163v4
  (11 December 2007), equation (15) p.15; Theorem 1.17 p.17; Corollary
  2.17 p.30; Proposition 3.1 and Definition 3.2 pp.35--36; §3.3
  pp.40--43, Definitions 3.8--3.9; Theorem 3.11 pp.44--46. Primary PDF
  directly read, including the period and Euler-factor formulas. Local
  source \path{work/h9/BK2007.pdf/.txt}.
\item
  Castella, \emph{Tamagawa number conjecture for CM modular forms and
  Rankin--Selberg convolutions}, 14 February 2025 manuscript, §2.1.1
  pp.5--6 (infinity types and reciprocity), §2.1.2 p.6 (periods),
  Theorem 2.2.1 pp.7--8 (the integral Katz function and uniqueness).
  This use is outside §5 and does not invoke its exact-conductor
  hypothesis.
\item
  BDP, \emph{p-adic L-functions and the coniveau filtration on Chow
  groups}, §5.2, for the character of \(y^2=x^3+x\), its conductor and
  the quadratic twist relation.
\item
  The rational algebra in (AR19.3)--(AR19.5) is also checked by the
  reproducible exact script \texttt{fermat\_katz\_coefficient.py}; no
  floating-point arithmetic is used.
\end{itemize}
% Proof-level conductor-scope repair for the next arithmetic gate.
% Run JAC1964-20261121; /root/h9_height; GPT-6 Astra Pro.
% Coordinate with AR19 (Katz value); this is H9-T15, not a Chow-rank theorem.

\subsection*{H9-T15. The conductor-general rank-one implication for the Fermat character}

\paragraph{Why a separate deduction is needed.}
Castella's \emph{Tamagawa number conjecture for CM modular forms and
Rankin--Selberg convolutions}, manuscript of 14 February 2025,
introduces in Section 5, printed p.~21, the standing hypothesis
$\mathfrak d_K\Vert\mathfrak c$. The displayed Theorem 5.2.1,
printed p.~23, does not repeat that hypothesis. It is therefore
inappropriate to disregard it merely by reading the theorem display.
For the Fermat character its conductor is $(1+i)^4$, while the
different of $\mathbf Q(i)$ is $(1+i)^2$; exact divisibility fails.
The argument below instead uses the arbitrary-conductor reciprocity
theorem of Section 4 and the Euler-system bound. It records exactly
the portion of the proof of Theorem 5.2.1 that does not use the
Section 5 good-pair or conductor hypothesis.

\begin{proposition}[H9-T15]
Let $K_0=\mathbf Q(i)$, let $p=5$, and let
$\chi=\psi^3\mu=(\psi')^3$ be the ideal-valued Hecke character of
the Fermat rank-two motive, in the notation of AR-11 and BDP
Section 5.2. Write $\lambda$ for this same character in Castella's
infinity-type convention, so that it has type $(-3,0)$ and central
character $\eta_{K_0}$. Put
\[
 \varphi=\lambda\mathbf N^{-2},\qquad
 V=V_\lambda(2).
\]
Thus $\varphi$ has type $(-1,2)$ and is self-dual in Castella's
Definition 2.2.2. Fix one embedding $K_0\hookrightarrow\mathbf Q_5$
and use the associated prime $v$ and its conjugate $\bar v$ as in
that source. Let $\Phi/\mathbf Q_5$ contain the character values
and let $T\subset V$ be a stable rank-one lattice over its integers
$\mathcal O$. One can take $\Phi=\mathbf Q_5$ in this example.

If
\[
                  \mathcal L_v(\varphi)(0)\ne0,              \tag{H9.56}
\]
then
\[
 \dim_\Phi\operatorname{Sel}_{\rm BK}(K_0,V)=1.             \tag{H9.57}
\]
This implication requires no exact-divisibility hypothesis at $2$.
It is a statement about this specified Selmer group; it does not
identify its dimension with a rational Chow rank.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We use the conventions of Castella throughout. In particular the
cyclotomic character has Hodge--Tate weight $+1$, and the two
Hodge--Tate weights of $V$ are $-1$ at $v$ and $2$ at $\bar v$.
Self-duality identifies its Tate dual $V^*(1)$ with the conjugate
rank-one representation $V^\tau$. Neither representation is
trivial globally or at either prime over $5$. Their local
Hodge--Tate weights are nonzero, which also proves
\[
 H^0(K_0,V)=H^0(K_0,V^*(1))=0,
 \qquad H^0((K_0)_w,V^*(1))=0\quad(w\mid5).                \tag{H9.58}
\]

\emph{The nonzero class and its local conditions.}
Castella's Theorem 4.2.1, printed p.~16, is stated for an arbitrary
self-dual Hecke character. Its elliptic-unit construction starts
with any prime-to-$p$ conductor ideal; it has no requirement that
the conductor have exact valuation equal to the different at a
ramified prime. Apply this construction to $\varphi$ and its
conjugate. Let
\[
 c\in H^1_{\rm Iw}(K_\infty,T),\qquad
 c_0\in H^1(K_0,T)
\]
be the normalized twisted elliptic-unit class and its trivial
anticyclotomic specialization. The specialization of the Coleman
maps, which interpolate the Bloch--Kato logarithm and dual
exponential in Theorem 4.2.1, gives the two consequences used in
the first half of Castella's printed p.~24 proof:
\[
 \mathcal L_v(\varphi)(0)\ne0
       \ \Longrightarrow\ \operatorname{loc}_{\bar v}(c_0)\ne0,
                                                               \tag{H9.59}
\]
and
\[
 \mathcal L_v(\varphi^\tau)(0)=0
       \ \Longrightarrow\ \operatorname{loc}_{v}(c_0)=0.
                                                               \tag{H9.60}
\]
These are the reciprocity and specialization statements of the
Section 4 theorem, not an invocation of the Section 5 conductor
hypothesis. Conjugating the two primes gives
$\mathcal L_{\bar v}(\varphi^\tau)(0)=\mathcal L_v(\varphi)(0)$
up to the compatible nonzero period unit, explaining the prime
$\bar v$ in (H9.59). The local weights $-1,2$ avoid the exceptional
weight-zero and weight-one cases; after inverting $5$ the relevant
logarithm or dual exponential identifies the one-dimensional local
space in question with its de Rham target.

The conjugate character has type $(2,-1)$, in the interpolation
range of Castella Theorem 2.2.1, printed p.~7. The central root
number of $\lambda$ is $-1$ by the Fermat character computation
in AR-11, so $L(\lambda,2)=L(\lambda^\tau,2)=0$.
Interpolation therefore gives
$\mathcal L_v(\varphi^\tau)(0)=0$, and (H9.60) applies.
Twisted elliptic units are unramified in the required rational
local condition away from $5$. At $5$, Lemma 3.4.2, printed p.~12,
identifies the Bloch--Kato local space with $0$ at $v$ and with
all of $H^1((K_0)_{\bar v},V)$ at $\bar v$. Consequently
\[
       0\ne c_0\otimes1
             \in\operatorname{Sel}_{\rm BK}(K_0,V).        \tag{H9.61}
\]

\emph{The Euler-system bound with the ramified prime retained.}
For clarity, the Euler system is taken over the ray-class tower
containing $K_0(\mathfrak c5^\infty)$ and all auxiliary ray-class
extensions at primes away from $6\mathfrak c5$, where
$\mathfrak c=(1+i)^4$. One starts with the elliptic units for
$\mathcal O(1)$ and twists by the rank-one character
$T\otimes\mathcal O(-1)$. This character factors through a finite
extension of the compositum of the $\mathbf Z_5$-extensions of
$K_0$, inside the displayed ray-class tower.

Rubin's twisting theorem constructs the full twisted Euler system
whose normalized base specialization is $c_0$: in the directly read author draft
\emph{Euler systems}, Chapter VI, Definition 3.1 and Theorem 3.5,
printed pp.~92--93, the new non-$p$ conductor is simply adjoined
to the excluded-prime ideal. Thus the prime above $2$ is among
the excluded bad primes. No constraint on its conductor exponent
is used. This is the same twisting input cited in Castella's
printed p.~24 proof as the published Rubin Theorem 6.4.1.
The harmless normalization of the elliptic units is the invertible
auxiliary-ideal factor in Castella Theorem 4.2.1: its proof explicitly
chooses $\mathfrak a$ prime to $6\mathfrak cp$ such that
$\sigma_{\mathfrak a}-\varphi^\tau(\sigma_{\mathfrak a})$
is invertible in the anticyclotomic Iwasawa algebra. More precisely,
apply Rubin to the \emph{unnormalized} full twisted elliptic-unit
Euler system. Its base class is the nonzero unit multiple of $c_0$
obtained by specializing that auxiliary-ideal factor. Thus one need
not invert the anticyclotomic normalization simultaneously on all
auxiliary ray extensions; its base specialization already supplies
the nonzero Euler-system class required below.

Rubin's Euler-system bound now gives
\[
           \operatorname{Sel}_{\rm str}(K_0,V^*(1))=0.
                                                               \tag{H9.62}
\]
The exact primary statement is Chapter II, Theorem 2.3, printed
p.~24 of that draft (published Theorem 2.2.3): a nonzero
Euler-system class for a nontrivial representation satisfying
$\operatorname{Hyp}(K_0,V)$ makes the strict dual torsion Selmer
group finite, and hence its rational space zero. Its hypotheses
hold for this rank-one representation: choose $\tau=1$ in the
rank-one-quotient condition, and irreducibility is automatic.
The same page's Remark 2.4 explicitly records this rank-one case.
The nontriviality condition follows also from (H9.58).

\emph{The dimension calculation.}
Let $\operatorname{Sel}_{\rm rel}(K_0,V)$ impose the Bloch--Kato
unramified condition away from $5$ and impose no condition at the
two primes over $5$. Poitou--Tate duality and the global Euler
characteristic give, using (H9.58),
\[
 \dim_\Phi\operatorname{Sel}_{\rm rel}(K_0,V)
    =d^-(V)+
       \dim_\Phi\operatorname{Sel}_{\rm str}(K_0,V^*(1)).    \tag{H9.63}
\]
Here $d^-(V)=1$, because $K_0$ has one complex place and $V$ has
rank one. A complete displayed derivation of (H9.63) is
Loeffler--Zerbes, \emph{Euler systems with local conditions},
arXiv:1710.04956v2, Proposition 2.8 and its proof, printed p.~3.
The only suppressed terms in (H9.63) are the global and local
$H^0$ terms explicitly checked in (H9.58).

By (H9.62), the relaxed space in (H9.63) has dimension one.
Its Bloch--Kato subspace contains the nonzero vector (H9.61).
It is therefore one-dimensional, which proves (H9.57).
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Scope of this check.}
This is a proof-level application of the arbitrary-conductor
reciprocity construction and rank-one Euler-system machinery.
It repairs the source-scope issue caused by the standing
Section 5 assumption; it does not claim that assumption was
explicitly withdrawn in the source. The hypothesis (H9.56)
remains a separate Katz-value calculation, assigned to AR19.
Neither a nonzero complex derivative alone nor the BDP
Rankin--Katz formula outside its stated ramification hypotheses
is used to prove it here. No scalar-extended Abel--Jacobi
injectivity, rational Chow-generation theorem, or positivity on
the full arithmetic complementary Chow group follows just from
(H9.57).

\paragraph{Primary sources directly inspected.}
Castella: \url{https://web.math.ucsb.edu/~castella/Katz.pdf},
Section 4.2 and printed p.~24 (the latter also visually inspected).
Rubin author draft:
\url{https://swc-math.github.io/aws/1999/99RubinES.pdf},
Chapter II pp.~23--24 and Chapter VI pp.~92--94.
Loeffler--Zerbes:
\url{https://discovery.ucl.ac.uk/id/eprint/10067153/1/1710.04956v2.pdf},
Theorem 2.5, Proposition 2.8 and its proof, printed pp.~2--3.
The general Euler-system theorems are used as established inputs;
they have not been reproved or formally verified in this run.

\subsection*{AR-16a. The remaining injectivity question over \(\mathbf Q\)}

Use the actual \(\mathbf Q\)-motive \(N\), rational class \(\eta\),
and \(F=\mathbf Q(i)\)-defined operation \(T\) of JAC-P17.
Put
\[
 V_L=\operatorname{CH}^2(N_L)_{\mathbf Q},\qquad
 W=H^3_{\mathrm{et}}(N_{\overline{\mathbf Q}},\mathbf Q_5)(2),
 \qquad
 S_L=H^1_f(L,W),
\]
and write \(\alpha_L:V_L\otimes_{\mathbf Q}\mathbf Q_5\to S_L\)
for the scalar-extended arithmetic Abel--Jacobi map.

\begin{corollary}[AR-16a]\label{ar16a}
The established AR-19, H9-T15, and JAC-P17 give
\[
                   \dim_{\mathbf Q_5}S_{\mathbf Q}=1,
       \qquad      \dim_{\mathbf Q_5}S_F=2.                \tag{AR16a.1}
\]
Moreover the following statements are equivalent:
\[
             \ker\alpha_{\mathbf Q}=0
                    \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
             \ker\alpha_F=0.                             \tag{AR16a.2}
\]
If these equivalent injectivity statements hold, then
\[
             V_{\mathbf Q}=\mathbf Q\eta,
       \qquad V_F=\mathbf Q\eta\oplus\mathbf Q T\eta.
                                                               \tag{AR16a.3}
\]
In that case the real height is strictly positive on the full
complementary Chow group over both fields. Without the
injectivity hypothesis, (AR16a.1) is a Selmer-dimension theorem
and (AR16a.3) has not been proved.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
JAC-P17 identifies the \(\mathbf Q\)-realization with the
representation induced from the Gaussian character
\(\chi=\psi_+^3\). AR-19 proves the nonzero Katz premise and
H9-T15 gives a one-dimensional Bloch--Kato Selmer group for the
corresponding one-dimensional representation over \(F\).
Shapiro's lemma identifies this with \(S_{\mathbf Q}\), giving
its dimension one. Restricting the induced representation to
\(F\) splits it into the two conjugate character representations.
Conjugation identifies the dimensions of their Selmer groups,
so \(\dim S_F=2\).

For completeness the kernel comparison is an actual descent
argument, not a rank heuristic. Let \(c\) be the nontrivial
element of \(\operatorname{Gal}(F/\mathbf Q)\).
Restriction--corestriction in Galois cohomology identifies
\(S_{\mathbf Q}\) with \(S_F^{c=1}\).
The Bloch--Kato local conditions are preserved by restriction
and corestriction, so the same averaging argument applies to
these Selmer subspaces; division by two is allowed in
\(\mathbf Q_5\). Correspondence functoriality and
\(c(T)=-T\) give
\[
                S_F=S_{\mathbf Q}\oplus T S_{\mathbf Q}.
                                                               \tag{AR16a.4}
\]
Indeed \(T^2=-1\) exchanges the two eigenspaces of \(c\),
exactly as for the Chow group in JAC-P17.

The Abel--Jacobi map commutes with restriction, corestriction,
and \(T\). Under JAC-P17's rational Chow decomposition and
(AR16a.4), its kernel consequently decomposes as
\[
 \ker\alpha_F=
       \operatorname{res}(\ker\alpha_{\mathbf Q})
          \oplus T\operatorname{res}(\ker\alpha_{\mathbf Q}).
                                                               \tag{AR16a.5}
\]
This proves (AR16a.2).

If \(\alpha_{\mathbf Q}\) is injective, (AR16a.1) implies
\(\dim_{\mathbf Q}V_{\mathbf Q}\le1\) by extension of scalars.
AR-13 supplies the nonzero class \(\eta\), so it is a basis.
JAC-P17 then gives the second equality in (AR16a.3).
The known height matrices are
\[
 h_{\mathbf Q}(r\eta,r\eta)=r^2h_{\mathbf Q}(\eta,\eta),
 \qquad
 h_F(r\eta+sT\eta,r\eta+sT\eta)
       =(r^2+s^2)h_{\mathbf Q}(\eta,\eta),
\]
with \(h_{\mathbf Q}(\eta,\eta)>1/100\).
Both forms are strictly positive on nonzero vectors.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Scope of the remaining question.}
The scalar extension in \(\alpha_L\) is indispensable, as
explained in AR-16. The corollary makes no uniform assertion
over further extensions of \(F\); new cycles and new Selmer
classes can occur there. It also does not identify a
\(p\)-adic Abel--Jacobi kernel with the radical of the real
height. Thus the numerical rank and field-of-definition
problems are resolved for these two fields, while the
Chow-theoretic injectivity requirement remains explicit.
% JAC1964-20261121, /root/arithmetic_sign.  Bloch input plus proved Selmer ranks.
\subsection*{AR-23. The positive Fermat classes span the actual Selmer image}

\begin{proposition}[AR-23]
Use the actual rational Fermat motive $N$ and class $\eta$ of JAC-P17,
and put $F=\mathbf Q(i)$,
\[
 V_k=\operatorname{CH}^2(N_k)_{\mathbf Q},\qquad
 W_5=H^3_{\mathrm{et}}(N_{\overline{\mathbf Q}},\mathbf Q_5)(2),
 \qquad S_k=H^1_f(k,W_5).
\]
Let
$\alpha_k:V_k\otimes_{\mathbf Q}\mathbf Q_5\to S_k$
be the scalar-extended etale Abel--Jacobi map used in AR-16a.
Then
\[
 \alpha_{\mathbf Q}(\eta)\ne0,\qquad
 S_{\mathbf Q}=\mathbf Q_5\alpha_{\mathbf Q}(\eta),
 \qquad
 S_F=\mathbf Q_5\alpha_F(\eta)
       \oplus\mathbf Q_5\alpha_F(T\eta).              \tag{AR.23a}
\]
In particular these regulators are surjective.  There are direct-sum
decompositions of $\mathbf Q_5$-vector spaces
\[
 \begin{split}
 V_{\mathbf Q}\otimes\mathbf Q_5
   &=\mathbf Q_5\eta\oplus\ker\alpha_{\mathbf Q},\\
 V_F\otimes\mathbf Q_5
   &=(\mathbf Q_5\eta\oplus\mathbf Q_5 T\eta)
                                      \oplus\ker\alpha_F.
 \end{split}                                                   \tag{AR.23b}
\]
No rational Chow splitting along these kernels, no vanishing of the
kernels, and no real-height orthogonality of the kernels is asserted.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We first use more of Bloch's original argument than just the stated
infinite-order conclusion.  In \emph{Algebraic cycles and values of
$L$-functions}, J. reine angew. Math. \textbf{350} (1984), \S4,
p.~104, Bloch writes the projected integral Abel--Jacobi class
\[
 \beta_5\in H^1(F,M(\mathbf Z_5(2)))
\]
of the Fermat Ceresa cycle and states that it is nonzero.  Here $M$ is
the rank-two summand of types $(3,0),(0,3)$.  On the same page he proves
\[
 H^0\bigl(F,M(\mathbf Q_5/\mathbf Z_5(2))\bigr)=0
\]
using Frobenius at $11$.  The long exact cohomology sequence of the
lattice inclusion therefore makes
\[
 H^1(F,M(\mathbf Z_5(2)))\longrightarrow
 H^1(F,M(\mathbf Q_5(2)))
\]
injective.  Thus his nonzero integral class has nonzero rational
Abel--Jacobi image; this is not merely a torsion-valued nonvanishing.

The cohomological projector used by Bloch and the realization of our
$\pi_N=\pi_3p_G$ have the same image: the extreme Hodge types.  The
complement has only types $(2,1),(1,2)$, so there is no Hodge morphism
in either direction between these two parts.  Consequently the
projection onto the extreme part is unique, and the two projected
rational Abel--Jacobi classes agree under this identification.  The
algebraic projectors and comparison isomorphisms carry this equality
to the $5$-adic realization.

We may take the hyperflex Abel embedding used in AR-8.  Bloch's
Theorem (3.4), p.~102, treats the Ceresa cycle for a choice of
embedding.  Independence of the projected Abel--Jacobi image from
that choice can also be checked directly.  A change of embedding is
a translation, and its change of cycle is algebraically trivial.
Over a finite extension it is a sum of images of degree-zero
divisors on curves.  After projection to $N$, each corresponding
map $H^1(D)\to H^3(N)(1)$ is zero by Hodge type, hence also on
etale cohomology by comparison.  Functoriality of Abel--Jacobi kills
the resulting projected image.  Rational restriction to a finite
extension is injective by restriction--corestriction, so this
conclusion descends to $F$.

For that embedding, let $X_1$ be its Beauville grade-one curve class.
By AR-7 and AR-8,
\[
 \kappa=[C]-[-1]_*[C]=2X_1,
 \qquad \eta=\mathcal F(X_1),\qquad p_G\kappa=\kappa.
\]
The middle component of the Fourier correspondence is
$c_1(\mathcal P)^3/3!$ on $J\times\widehat J$; after the principal
polarization it induces an invertible map on $H^3(J)$ and on its
extreme summand.  Fourier commutes with $G$ because $G$ preserves
the polarization.  Functoriality now gives
\[
 \alpha_F(\eta)=\tfrac12\mathcal F_*\beta_5\ne0
\]
after rationalization and the indicated identifications.  Only
invertibility is needed here; no integral lattice index or unit
normalization of this Fourier map is asserted.

JAC-P17 places $N$ and $\eta$ over $\mathbf Q$ and identifies their
realization with the precise Hecke character used in AR-19 and
H9-T15.  Since restriction takes
$\alpha_{\mathbf Q}(\eta)$ to the just-proved nonzero class,
$\alpha_{\mathbf Q}(\eta)\ne0$.  The proven Selmer dimensions are
\[
 \dim_{\mathbf Q_5}S_{\mathbf Q}=1,\qquad
 \dim_{\mathbf Q_5}S_F=2.
\]
Complex conjugation $c$ fixes $\eta$, and $cT=-Tc$, $T^2=-1$.
The same identities hold on Selmer classes by functoriality.
Therefore $\alpha_F(\eta)$ and $\alpha_F(T\eta)$ are nonzero and
belong to the distinct $c=+1$ and $c=-1$ eigenspaces.  They are
$\mathbf Q_5$-linearly independent, proving (AR.23a).

Finally, for either field, the regulator restricts to an isomorphism
from the displayed $\mathbf Q_5$-span of the Fermat classes onto
$S_k$.  Subtracting the unique element of that span with the same
regulator value gives (AR.23b), including uniqueness.  This is
linear algebra over $\mathbf Q_5$ and does not use finite
dimensionality of the source Chow group.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Remaining arithmetic gap.}
The known positive classes now account for the entire Selmer target,
not just for some abstract subspace of the Chow group.  The remaining
issue is whether the scalar-extended Abel--Jacobi kernels in
(AR.23b) vanish, or whether a separately proved real-height statement
controls all additional classes.  Even a surjective map to a
one-dimensional $\mathbf Q_5$-space permits many independent rational
Chow classes, and the coefficients of their Selmer projection need
not be rational.  The real height pairing cannot be extended
canonically to $\mathbf Q_5$, so (AR.23b) is not an orthogonal
decomposition for that pairing.  In particular no real radical
factorization follows from this proposition.

There is no conflict with AR-20's vanishing of rational finite-field
specializations.  Bloch's detection keeps integral and torsion
cohomological information before rationalizing the global
Abel--Jacobi class.  The ordinary rational Chow class of each
specialized odd-degree motive is a different invariant and is zero
in AR-20.

\paragraph{Exact source scope.}
Bloch's original p.~104 was visually checked, including the integral
class, its nonvanishing, and the vanishing of the torsion-module
invariants; Theorem (3.4) and its embedding discussion are on p.~102.
The proof above transports that established class through the actual
Fermat projector and Fourier correspondence and combines it with the
new Selmer calculation.  The image in $H^1_f$ and functoriality are
the same etale Abel--Jacobi formalism already used in AR-16a; no
conjectural injectivity is added.  Primary scan:
\url{https://gdz.sub.uni-goettingen.de/fulltext/PPN243919689_0350/00000110?_format=xml}.
% JAC1964-20261121. /root/arithmetic_sign; GPT-6 Astra Pro.
\subsection*{AR-20. Why finite-field Chow injectivity does not detect the arithmetic complement}

\begin{lemma}[AR-20a]\label{ar20a}
Let \(B/\mathbf F_q\) be an abelian variety, let \(h^j(B)\) be its
canonical Chow--K\"unneth summand, and let \(r\) be an integer.
Then
\[
     \operatorname{CH}^r(h^j(B))_{\mathbf Q}=0
                     \qquad\text{if }j\ne2r.                 \tag{AR20.1}
\]
This assertion uses the known finite-dimensionality of abelian
Chow motives and the Weil bounds; it does not require the Tate
conjecture.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let \(F:B\to B\) be the absolute \(q\)-power Frobenius, which is a
morphism over \(\mathbf F_q\), and write \(F^*\) for its action on
the Chow motive. The canonical projectors commute with \(F^*\).
Let \(P_j(t)\in\mathbf Q[t]\) be the characteristic polynomial of
\(F^*\) on \(H^j(B_{\overline{\mathbf F}_q},\mathbf Q_\ell)\),
where \(\ell\nmid q\). This polynomial has rational coefficients:
it is the appropriate exterior-power polynomial of the Weil
polynomial on \(H^1\). Every complex root of \(P_j\) has absolute
value \(q^{j/2}\).

The endomorphism \(P_j(F^*)\) of \(h^j(B)\) is homologically
trivial, hence numerically trivial. Since \(h^j(B)\) is a
finite-dimensional Chow motive, Kimura nilpotence gives an integer
\(a>0\) such that
\[
                    P_j(F^*)^a=0
             \quad\text{in }\operatorname{End}(h^j(B)).
             \tag{AR20.2}
\]
On codimension-\(r\) cycles over \(\mathbf F_q\), flat pullback by
the absolute \(q\)-power Frobenius is multiplication by \(q^r\).
Indeed Frobenius fixes every underlying integral subvariety and
its pullback has multiplicity \(q^r\) in codimension \(r\).
Thus for \(z\in\operatorname{CH}^r(h^j(B))_{\mathbf Q}\),
(AR20.2) gives
\[
                        P_j(q^r)^a z=0.
\]
When \(j\ne2r\), the Weil absolute values show that
\(P_j(q^r)\ne0\). It is an invertible rational scalar, so \(z=0\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[AR-20b]\label{ar20b}
Let \(J/K_0\) be the Fermat Jacobian, with
\(K_0=\mathbf Q(i)\), and let \(N\subset h^3(J)\) be the
canonical complement. At every finite place of good reduction
where these projectors are spread out, the specialization map
\[
 \operatorname{sp}_v:
 \operatorname{CH}^2(N_{K_0})_{\mathbf Q}
       \longrightarrow
 \operatorname{CH}^2(N_{k(v)})_{\mathbf Q}
                                                               \tag{AR20.3}
\]
is the zero map, since its target is zero. The same holds after
any finite extension of the number field.
In particular the nonzero, positive-height classes
\(\eta,T\eta\) of AR-13 and JAC-P11 specialize to zero rationally
at all such places.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Specialization for a smooth proper model respects correspondence
actions and the canonical abelian Chow--K\"unneth projectors.
It therefore takes the displayed group into
\(\operatorname{CH}^2(h^3(J_{k(v)}))_{\mathbf Q}\), which is zero
by AR-20a with \(j=3\) and \(r=2\). If a chosen correspondence
requires a finite residue extension, apply the result there and
use restriction--corestriction on rational Chow groups.
The global classes \(\eta,T\eta\) are nonzero by their positive
height matrix. Thus they are explicit nonzero elements in the
kernel of the product of all these specialization maps.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Exact limitation of the specialization route.}
JAC-P15's finite-field rational--numerical comparison cannot
be promoted to injectivity of the arithmetic Abel--Jacobi map
by testing these finite fibres: all of the arithmetic complement
already disappears in their rational Chow groups. No failure of
JAC-P15 is involved. The missing information concerns the
number-field extension class and its global regulator.

This limitation also appears in the general cycle-class
comparison. Kahn, \emph{On the injectivity and non-injectivity of
the \(l\)-adic cycle class maps}, arXiv:2307.13281v2
(10 April 2024), Section~4, printed pp.~10--12, explains that
passing from Chow groups to completed \'{e}tale motivic Chow
groups may acquire kernel even in an attempted argument which
assumes finite generation of ordinary Chow groups. His
Theorem~5.1, printed p.~13, derives the positive-characteristic
statement from the full Tate--Beilinson conjecture, while
Section~5.7, printed pp.~17--18, does not give a
characteristic-zero analogue. The decomposition-of-the-diagonal
criterion in Theorem~6.1, printed p.~18, assumes
\(\operatorname{CH}_0(X_{k(X)})_{\mathbf Q}=\mathbf Q\);
it is not available for \(J\). In particular a factorization of
the whole summand \(N(1)\) through degree-one motives would
contradict its Hodge types \((2,-1),(-1,2)\).

\paragraph{Established inputs and check scope.}
The nilpotence mechanism is the same one explicitly proved in
Kahn 2003, Proposition~1.5 and Lemma~1.6, printed p.~981, and
used in JAC-P13 and JAC-P15. AR-20 applies that mechanism to
the actual Frobenius polynomial before any Tate-conjecture
input. The Chow--K\"unneth decomposition and its functoriality
are the established abelian-motive inputs already used in
AR-1--AR-2. No claim about a nonzero zero-height class or a
negative-height class is made here.
\subsection*{Source audit: Murre's theorem does not remove the Fermat gate}

Vial, \emph{Niveau and coniveau filtrations on cohomology groups
and Chow groups}, author manuscript of 19 March 2012,
Theorem 2(5)--(6), pp.~4--5, and Theorem 4.8,
Corollary 4.10, Remark 4.11, p.~24, were read directly at
\url{https://www.math.uni-bielefeld.de/~vial/coniveau11.pdf}.
Corollary 4.10 establishes Murre (A), (B), (C$'$), (D) for abelian
threefolds. Theorem 2(6) identifies the kernel of the
\emph{algebraically trivial} Abel--Jacobi map with the kernel
of the appropriate niveau projector. These statements do not
assert injectivity of the arithmetic map in AR-16a.

In our application the unresolved cycles belong to
$\operatorname{CH}^2_{(1)}(J)$, specifically the summand $N$.
Murre(D), or the grade-zero divisor/Fourier argument in AR-1a,
does not annihilate this degree-one summand. The stronger
additional condition on odd cohomology in Theorem 4.8 would require
$H^3$ to be generated by degree-one cohomology of curves with its
twist. It fails on $H(N)$, whose types are $(3,0),(0,3)$.
An algebraic curve correspondence instead has image of types
$(2,1),(1,2)$. Thus the source's extra hypothesis is unavailable.

The distinction is also visible in AR-15a: its possible
grade-zero obstruction lies on $B\times J$ for Jacobians $B$
of unbounded dimension, not on the threefold $J$ alone.
Neither Corollary 4.10 nor the threefold Fourier calculation
supplies grade-zero injectivity on all these products.
This is a scope check of cited background, not a new theorem
or a claim that the remaining injectivity statement is false.
\part{Unresolved assertions and exploratory work}
\section{What remains to be proved}
The full primitive arithmetic height form on the complementary CM Chow
motive remains to be controlled. A formula for one special cycle does
not prove positivity for every cycle. Analytic derivative signs require
an applicable real self-dual $L$-function and a proved height identity
with its local terms and positive constants. The Bost slope inequality
requires an actual canonical height line and a proved norm estimate.
Neither missing input is silently supplied by purity or Dirichlet GRH.
For the specified Fermat class the height sign is now proved directly,
independently of any $L'$-height identity. The remaining Fermat gate is
positivity on its entire Griffiths quotient, together with vanishing
of its algebraically trivial subgroup if strict positivity on rational
Chow is required. The positive CM plane is known, but a spanning
theorem for the whole group has not been proved. The full abelian
endomorphism orbit cannot enlarge that plane. JAC-P17 and AR-16a
make the remaining sufficient gate precise over $\mathbf Q$: injectivity
of the scalar-extended arithmetic Abel--Jacobi map into the proved
one-dimensional Bloch--Kato Selmer group. This is equivalent to the
corresponding injectivity over $\mathbf Q(i)$. All finite-field
specializations of this complementary Chow group vanish by AR-20,
so finite-fibre injectivity cannot supply this missing arithmetic step.

The geometric H9 assertions cannot be completed in their original form:
the counterexamples above refute the general formula and the asserted
incidence domain. For abelian ambient varieties condition A is automatic.
The full geometric height problem reduces to an explicit primitive
intersection form on the axis. It closes for every axis in characteristic
zero by H9-T8a, for threefold fibres in every characteristic, and for
geometric generic axes in every dimension covered by JAC-P09. These
statements extend through purely inseparable field extensions. For
finite separable covers the old subspace splits orthogonally from the
new norm-zero subspace. H9-T17 refutes positivity on the whole new
homological group, with an explicit negative Lefschetz class of
self-height $-12d^3$. In characteristic zero for threefold fibres,
Zhang's theorem instead gives positivity on the admissible
Albanese-primitive subgroup; its orthogonal Lefschetz subgroup has
the negative Picard height. The remaining cover questions must retain
this primitive qualification and their characteristic and admissibility
hypotheses. The algebraically trivial primitive incidence domain is
controlled after every finite extension.
